The central oval vignette depicts a white "pelican in its piety"—a mythical image of a pelican plucking its own breast to feed its chicks with its blood. In alchemy, this symbolizes the "work of sacrifice" or the liquid stage of the alchemical process. The pelican sits on a nest atop a shield divided into four sections, each containing a reddish-brown rose or four-leaf design.</image-desc>

*ever conceived the thought of offering*

<summary>The author continues his humble dedication, arguing that just as great celestial bodies can be represented by small marks, his small book serves as a token of his great gratitude. He notes that while he is a man of the Church (Grace), the book's subject (Nature/Alchemy) is outside his usual professional scope.</summary>

*into smoke.* <note>Completing the thought from the previous page: bad alchemists turn gold into smoke rather than the other way around.</note> *It is the work* <note>original: "Ouurage"</note> *of a young man who was once very close to me, and who died recently with the reputation of a man of virtue and learning, whose writings fell into my hands. And as I read through this one quite attentively, it seemed to me that in its brevity, it encompassed much, and could at least disabuse the "Blowers"* <note>original: "Souffleurs," a derisive term for amateur or greedy alchemists who focused only on the physical bellows and furnace rather than the philosophical or spiritual aspects of the craft.</note> *and unfortunate Alchemists of these times, provided they are not among the most stubborn.* <note>original: "opiniastres"</note> *I expect that you will find the style a little neglected, bearing witness to the haste of its Author; nevertheless, it possesses a certain charm* <note>original: "gentillesse," implying a nobility or elegance of spirit.</note> *and a playfulness in its invention. And as for the substance, I certainly dare to promise myself that it will have your approval, that it will entertain you pleasantly for a few hours, and after all, that if the goodness of your mind* <note>original: "Esprit"</note> *—which is in no way common—is not*

# TO THE PHILOSOPHER
## Unknown. <note>The "Unknown Philosopher" (Philosophe Inconnu) is a common trope in hermetic and alchemical literature, referring either to an anonymous author of a foundational text or to the idealized archetype of a sage who possesses secret knowledge of nature.</note>

# ODE.

Mysterious Philosopher,
Wise guide of the Seekers, <note>original: "Curieux." In the 17th century, this term referred to people with an active, scholarly interest in the hidden secrets of science, nature, and art.</note>
Interpreter of Nature,
Accept that the passion
That I feel for your work <note>original: "affection." In this context, it implies a deep intellectual and emotional devotion to the subject matter.</note>
Accompanies your venture.

Not that I dare to presume
To speak as well as I love;
My poetic vein cannot suffice
To offer the well-deserved verses
To the lofty qualities
Of this Work <term>Ouurage: used here to mean a significant intellectual or alchemical treatise</term> that I admire.

<vocab>Philosopher, Ode, Nature, Seeker, Work</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a celebratory poem dedicated to the "Unknown Philosopher," praising him as a guide to the secrets of nature. The author, Albert Belin, humbly expresses that his own poetic skills are insufficient to fully honor the greatness of the work he is presenting.</summary>
<keywords>Ode, Unknown Philosopher, Nature, Seekers, alchemical poetry, Albert Belin</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Ode to the Unknown Philosopher" begun on the previous page. Having established his own humility, Belin now addresses the reception of the Philosopher’s work, contrasting the inevitable criticism of the ignorant with the high esteem of the truly learned.</meta>

It is true that upon your writings
A world of small-minded spirits <note>original: "petits Esprits." This refers to narrow-minded critics or those lacking the spiritual insight required to understand hermetic philosophy.</note>
Will offer diverse censures;
And that which you prove the best
Will appear before their eyes
Only as an argument for controversies.

But their designs will be con-
founded,
When the most Learned <note>original: "Sçauans." In the 17th century, this term referred to the established community of scholars and scientists.</note> pay you
A thousand respectful homages,
And will judge that your labors
Are preferred above their own,
As if theirs were merely reflections. <note>original: "images." The poet suggests that the Philosopher's work is the original reality, while other scholars' works are merely faint imitations.</note>

They will see the happy successes
Of your adventurous paths,
Worthy of higher praises
Than those with which Antiquity
Consecrates to Immortality
The richest of the Argonauts. <note>The Argonauts, who sought the Golden Fleece, were a favorite symbol for alchemists. Their "adventurous path" was viewed as an allegory for the arduous and dangerous process of the Great Work.</note>

<sig>iii</sig>

<vocab>Spirits, Learned ones, Argonauts, Antiquity, Immortality, writings, censures, homages</vocab>

<summary>The poem concludes by predicting that while the "Unknown Philosopher" will face criticism from the narrow-minded, true scholars will recognize his work's superiority. The author compares the Philosopher’s achievement to the legendary quest for the Golden Fleece.</summary>
<keywords>Unknown Philosopher, alchemical allegory, Argonauts, scholarly reception, intellectual labor, Albert Belin</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Ode to the Unknown Philosopher" from the previous page. Having argued that true scholars will prefer this work to their own, the poet now praises the author’s selfless dedication to knowledge and his mastery over the secrets of Nature.</meta>

<margin>3.</margin>
They will approve of your fervor,
And the effects of your sincerity; <note>original: "candeur." In 17th-century spiritual and alchemical contexts, this implies both a moral purity and a "whiteness" of soul necessary for the Great Work.</note>
Your fervor to forever learn
And to increase your knowledge,
Your sincerity in possessing nothing
Except to give and share it.

</margin>
Thus your intellectual pursuits <note>original: "divertissemens." This suggests that what others find laborious, the philosopher performs as a noble and delightful exercise of the mind.</note>
Will become their great delights,
And your subtle discovery <note>original: "invention." In early modern French, this refers to the uncovering or "finding" of existing natural truths (from the Latin *invenire*).</note>
Will force them to maintain
That your Book knows how to contain
The delightful alongside the useful. <note>A reference to the Horatian ideal of "utile dulci"—the principle that a work should be both pleasing to the reader and practically instructive.</note>

That with a piercing eye you know
The cycles and the Laws
Of Nature’s most hidden realms;
And that no indiscretion <note>A key virtue in Hermetic philosophy: the "Unknown Philosopher" knows how to discuss these mysteries without "indiscreetly" revealing the secret of the Stone to the unworthy.</note>
Regarding her rich operation <note>original: "operation." This refers to the chemical and spiritual processes by which Nature effectuates change and perfection.</note>
Can ever be blamed upon you.

<vocab>fervor, sincerity, knowledge, intellectual pursuits, delights, Book, delightful, useful, Nature, operation</vocab>

<summary>The poem concludes by praising the author’s selfless desire to share his knowledge and his profound understanding of Nature's hidden laws. It asserts that the author has achieved the perfect balance of instruction and pleasure while maintaining the necessary silence regarding the most sacred secrets.</summary>
<keywords>fervor, sincerity, hidden laws of Nature, Horatian philosophy, Hermetic silence, alchemical operation</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Ode to the Unknown Philosopher," moving from the author's internal virtues to the external impact of his work on both misguided seekers and true scholars.</meta>

That by disabusing the Blowers, <note>original: "Souffleurs." A common derogatory term in the 17th century for "puffers"—alchemists who focused solely on the physical heat of the forge and the bellows to make gold, rather than the spiritual and natural philosophy of the craft.</note>
Who seek through so many misfortunes
The happiness of a miser, <note>A reference to those who pursue alchemy out of greed for material wealth rather than the "felicity" of wisdom.</note>
You teach them with good reason
Not to go in search of the Fleece <note>The "Golden Fleece" (Toison d'or) was a standard alchemical allegory representing the Philosopher's Stone or the successful completion of the Great Work.</note>
By a path that leads them astray.

In short, the great minds of the age
Will reread your writings with contentment,
And will grant them this endorsement:
That it belongs only to the envious
To insultingly despise
The merits of such a Work. <note>original: "Ouurage." This suggests both the physical book and the "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) of alchemical transformation.</note>

<vocab>Blowers, Miser, Fleece, Great Minds, Work</vocab>

<summary>This concluding section of the poem praises the author for redirecting greedy alchemists toward the true path and asserts that only the envious would fail to recognize the book's immense value.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Souffleurs, Golden Fleece, Philosopher's Stone, greed vs. wisdom, 17th-century poetry</keywords>

# THE ARGUMENT <note>In 17th-century literature, an "Argument" is a concise summary or synopsis of the plot or contents of a book or chapter.</note> OF THE
## First Book.

*This first Book declares how the <term>Unknown Philosopher</term> <note>original: "Philosophe inconneu." A common trope in alchemical literature referring to an anonymous adept; later, this became the specific pseudonym of the mystic Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin.</note> having heard talk of the wonders of the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term>, <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the elixir of life.</note> left his country, and having arrived in one of the largest cities in Europe, he made the acquaintance of a very elderly Lady, who claimed to know how to make the Stone of the Philosophers. This lady, having fallen in love with him, promised to reveal and perform the secret in his presence, on the condition that he would marry her, and that he would join the <term>so-called Reformed Religion</term> <note>original: "Religion pretenduë reformée." A standard 17th-century Catholic legal and polemical term for Protestantism (specifically the Huguenot faith in France).</note> which she had professed since her youth.*

<summary>This page provides a synopsis of the first book, introducing the protagonist—the Unknown Philosopher—and his encounter with an elderly woman who offers him the secret of alchemy in exchange for marriage and religious conversion.</summary>
<keywords>Unknown Philosopher, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, Reformed Religion, Protestantism, 17th-century narrative, conversion</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the synopsis of the first book, detailing the Philosopher's evasion of the lady's demands and the disastrous results of their alchemical collaboration.</meta>

which the Philosopher politely evaded by two clever subtleties, <note>original: "subtilitez." In this context, it refers to witty ruses or double-entendres used to dodge a difficult question.</note> and having misled and entertained her with these equivocal answers, he set her to <term>work</term>, <note>original: "ouurage." In alchemical texts, "the Work" (or Great Work) refers to the actual laboratory process of preparing the Philosopher's Stone.</note> during which time several unexpected accidents occurred, capable of ruining the entire project; one day the chimney fell, beneath which was the <term>vessel</term>, <note>original: "vaiffeau." This is the specialized glass or ceramic container—sometimes called the "Philosophical Egg"—in which the alchemical materials were heated.</note> and she, thinking to save it, received fourteen wounds. Afterwards, through the malice of the Philosopher’s confidant, it was carried off by one of his friends; unable to endure this, the said Philosopher took his revenge for it and changed countries, with the same intent of finding the secret. The whole is filled with merry intrigues.

<summary>The synopsis of the first book concludes with the Philosopher tricking the elderly lady, a laboratory disaster involving a collapsed chimney, and the Philosopher’s eventual flight to a new country to continue his quest.</summary>

->The Adventures of<-

that most of the spirits inhabiting it are quite insightful into its greatest secrets, and curious to examine all its workings, <note>original: "ressorts," referring to the hidden mechanisms or internal springs of nature.</note> particularly the method of composing the stone we call Philosophical. Among several whom I have known, a relative of mine, recently deceased, who was learned in the art and certain of the work, gave me my first thoughts toward seeking this great secret, which fools of all times have judged impossible; seeing as I often listened to him discourse upon it with such elegance and learning, <note>original: "gentillesse & de Doctrine." In this period, "gentillesse" referred to noble refinement or grace, while "Doctrine" signified formal, authoritative knowledge.</note> that I never left his company without receiving great desires to acquire the knowledge of it—rather for the beauty and rarity of the <term>Great Work (L'Oeuure): the allegorical and practical process of creating the Philosophers' Stone</term> than for the use of its rich effects, which I have always held in very little regard.

    This was the reason I resolved to undertake a journey, with the intent of consulting all those I knew to have a reputation for being good

->*The Unknown Philosopher.*<-

Philosophers: destiny led me to the most noble City of one of the most flourishing Kingdoms of the West, where I conferred with the most Learned. At each conference, my hopes took on new strength, and my desires to find the means to receive the fruit of my labor grew likewise. As I was searching everywhere to encounter some Angel Gabriel <note>A reference to the Archangel Gabriel, the messenger of divine revelations, signifying the narrator's search for a divine sign or a perfect teacher.</note> who might announce to me the news that my spirit anticipated, I learned that in a corner of a street lived a certain Lady of extreme Old Age and incomparable knowledge.

Of the greatest secrets of the World, she made her little pastimes and ordinary toys; she knew Medicine so well that gout, paralysis, dropsy <term>Dropsy (Hydropisie): an archaic medical term for edema, or the painful accumulation of fluids in the body.</term>, and other such illnesses commonly judged incurable were her minor cures. ### The Unknown Philosopher.

I gave credit to all the rest and assured myself that she possessed the **secret** <note>In alchemical literature, "the secret" refers to the "Grand Œuvre" (Great Work)—the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. Thus persuaded, you can imagine how I courted her favor; I visited her often, offering my service each time. I provided for her necessities as much as I could, and all with the intent of **winning her heart**, knowing well that since affection is blind, it would lead her to **break her silence** <note>original: "rompre la paille" — literally "to break the straw," an idiom meaning to break a formal agreement or a solemn vow of secrecy.</note> despite all the considerations that had prevented her from revealing this great secret.

I succeeded in this design; indeed, it was necessary to proceed in such a way, since once the cause was won, the effect could not fail me. Being thus assured of her **affection**, I spoke freely, and took the opportunity to seize my **prey** <note>The narrator uses predatory language here—"rauir ma proye"—to describe obtaining the alchemical knowledge.</note>.

"Madam," I said to her, "you often tell me that I am master of your affections; this is a point I do not understand among friends..."

Here, the "gentlemen" represents the idle or the uninitiated who benefit from the work of the dedicated.</note>, who sleep all...

### -> The Unknown Philosopher <-

Before long, the wind in our sails <note>original: "le vent en pouppe"; a nautical idiom meaning a favorable wind or having circumstances in one's favor.</note> led us to a very famous city where I had no acquaintances; nonetheless, I did not cease to converse as much as I could to learn the language. 

By good fortune, walking one day through the streets, I recognized a friend of mine, as delighted by our meeting as I was by his. If friends favor and cherish one another in their native country, how much more so in foreign lands; he quickly inquired why I had come. I had the thought to hide my purpose from him, but what means is there with a friend? For at the same moment that one wishes to hide something, at the same moment one no longer wishes to love. Truly, the law of friendship forbidding me to feign, commanded me to tell him everything. 

Having learned of this design of mine, he knew not what judgment to pass on me; he thought that some illness had unhinged my brain, for the Alchemists of the time <note>By the 17th century, the figure of the alchemist was often satirized in literature as a "gold-cook" or a madman who ruined himself with expensive and futile experiments.</note>—

# Les Auentures du <note>The full title of the work is likely *Les Aventures du Philosophe Inconnu* (The Adventures of the Unknown Philosopher).</note>

[Alchemists of the time] have so discredited the science,
that those who devote themselves to it
pass for madmen; is it not a
strange misfortune, that through the fault
of two or three Sophists, <note>In the 17th century, "Sophist" was a derogatory term for fraudulent alchemists who used complex, deceptive language to hide their lack of true knowledge.</note> wisdom

to foster his madness; but it is
also to nourish his friendship. What
shall I do?" Here are two contrary
motives: one hinders me, the other [constrains me]...

### Unknown Philosopher.

save me. Are you not yet persuaded? I have only a few words left to say to you: your own self-interest has not been able to turn you aside, and my respect for you cannot shake your resolve. You know all too well that a man who has undertaken this design <note>The "design" refers to the quest for the Philosopher's Stone.</note> no longer thinks of anything else; he is incapable of any other pursuit. # Unknown Philosopher 69

...as much as meeting him had caused me joy; as he was established and well-known in the city, I expected some assistance from him to achieve my <term>design</term> <note>The author's "design" or "plan" refers to his quest to find a master of alchemy and the secret of the Stone.</note>, but I saw immediately that he did not even approve of the thought of it; I could not stop myself from saying to him:

"Sir, I am extremely mistaken; I told you all my plans thinking that you would assist me by introducing me to some skilled <term>Philosopher</term> <note>In the 17th century, "Philosopher" was a common term for an alchemist or an "adept" who understood the secrets of nature.</note>, and yet you deploy all your eloquence to steer me away from my search. I am still in the harbor, and you want me to come ashore; even if my plan were blameworthy, would I be right to abandon it so soon after conceiving it? Would you not say yourself that I am <term>fickle</term>?" <note>Original: *volage*. A term implying someone who is inconsistent or lacks the "constancy" required for the Great Work of alchemy.</note>

"Ah, dear friend, one cannot withdraw from evil too soon."

"I do not know if it is evil; experience alone can make that known."

<summary>The narrator expresses his disappointment that his well-connected friend refuses to help him find an alchemical master, instead trying to persuade him to abandon his quest before he has even begun. The narrator defends his pursuit, arguing that he must experience it for himself rather than being judged as fickle for quitting too soon.</summary>

# The Adventures of... 70

It is a very bad <term>maxim</term> <note>A short statement expressing a general truth or rule of conduct.</note> to abandon an evil only once one feels its rigors; that is to act like those who lock the stable doors once the horses have been stolen. It is like those sick patients to whom the <term>Doctor</term> <note>Original: *Medecin*. 17th-century medical advice often focused on strict dietary "regimens" to balance the body's humors.</note> forbids certain meats; they only obey after they have already suffered the ill effects.

"According to you, then, one should never undertake anything unless one is certain of the outcome."

"That is one of the maxims established by <term>prudence</term>."

"But it is contrary to great <term>courage</term>."

"Rather, it is contrary to <term>temerity</term>." <note>Temerity refers to excessive confidence or reckless boldness.</note>

"So, you call 'temerity' what I call 'great courage.'"

"I give each thing its true name."

"Be that as it may, what you say is impossible; only <term>God</term> can be certain of what He undertakes."

"Absolutely, what you say..."

<summary>The dialogue continues with the friend arguing that waiting to experience the negative consequences of a pursuit before quitting is foolish. # The Unknown Philosopher 71

...is true; but when I say that one must be certain, I mean <term>morally</term> <note>In the 17th century, "moral certainty" referred to a level of proof sufficient for human action and practical life, distinguishing it from absolute, divine truth.</note> and not <term>metaphysically</term>, which I do not see in you; you are gambling with chance.

"It is a strange case to see good minds allow themselves to be carried away by the sentiment of the <term>common people</term>." <note>Original: *commun peuple*. The "Unknown Philosopher" often critiques those who follow popular opinion rather than the "hidden" truths of nature.</note>

"It is a hundred times stranger to provide those people with subjects of mockery."

"How opposed we are in our opinions."

"Yours is therefore quite false, since mine is true."

"How do you see that?"

"One would have to be blind [not to]."

"Will you remain in this error for long?"

"When will you abandon your <term>obstinacy</term>?" <note>In philosophical dialogues of this period, "obstinacy" (stubbornness) was considered a significant intellectual failing that prevented one from reaching the truth.</note>

"Truly, I pity you."

"And I feel great compassion for you."

"From what I can see, we shall never agree."

    The jealousy we feel regarding our secrets causes us to remain too withdrawn; we wish to possess this treasure <note>The "treasure" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the "Great Secret" mentioned on the previous page.</note> alone, and since each man believes he knows more than his companion, each also keeps himself hidden; without a doubt, this is why we remain ignorant for so long.
    In every kind of matter, when it is a question of being certain of a point, assemblies are necessary. Do you not see that to understand the illness of some person of rank, the regular Physician does not trust his own judgment, but a consultation is called? In matters of Religion, the <term>Doctors<gloss>In this context, "Doctors" refers to Doctors of Theology or high-ranking scholars of the Church who determined official doctrine.</gloss></term> dare not decide...

Let us not be jealous; this treasure is great enough for the twelve or thirteen of us who are here.

# The Unknown Philosopher.

The blessed hour had not yet struck in which God wished to rejoice me with this grace. But today, glory be to the Almighty, I have found this treasure; I know the <term>matter<gloss>The physical substance used as the starting point for alchemical transformation, often called the "Prima Materia."</gloss></term> and the manner of conducting it to the desired point—what more could you want? I do not regret any of my pains, since I now see them ended with a successful outcome; that which costs us the most is most agreeable to us when we finally possess it.

Learn then from me, and never doubt it, that the <term>Sun<gloss>In alchemical texts, "the Sun" (Latin: *Sol*) almost always refers to Gold.</gloss></term> is the true matter; reason will force you to admit it. **Gold** is the most perfect among all metals; it is the least combustible and corruptible, insofar as its parts are so pure and subtle, and so joined together, that the <term>earthy part<gloss>The solid, fixed element of a substance that provides its structure.</gloss></term> is protected from <note>The author is using the Aristotelian theory of the four elements (Earth, Air, Fire, Water), suggesting that gold's "moisture" acts as a binding agent that prevents fire from destroying its "earth."</note> combustion by its **humidity**, and its moisture is held so firmly by the...

### The Unknown Philosopher

### That one must work upon Saturn.

We wish to make **Gold**; it is therefore, without doubt, through the mediation of its father. One should not

Would that I were equally certain of going one day to Heaven.

possesses sulfur more fixed
than is required for itself; it
can therefore provide fixation
without loss <note>original: "sans interest." In this context, it suggests the metal can donate its "fixing" power without depleting its own essential nature.</note>.

This Paschal Virgin, without doubt, is Antimony <note>Antimony was a central substance in 17th-century alchemy, often called the "wolf of metals" because it "devoured" impurities. Believe me then, gentlemen, this is our matter; even if I were to die, I shall remain firm in this opinion.

I confess, Gentlemen, that Antimony is the matter of the Stone <term>The Stone<gloss>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary alchemical substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and achieving physical perfection.</gloss></term>, but not the whole of it. It is certain that three things are required; otherwise, the greatest Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to "Philosophers by Fire," or master alchemists.</note> have all been deceived. If a single subject possessed a good spirit, body, and soul, it would be a perfect compound; there would be none more noble, and therefore it could not be the "matter"—which, in the opinion of everyone, is very vile and of low price <note>A core alchemical tenet: the *materia prima* (prime matter) is something common and despised by the world, yet contains hidden divine potential.</note>. It was highly valued because, when distilled, it produced "spirit of vitriol" (sulfuric acid), a powerful solvent.</note> must provide its spirit, and Arsenic the soul; and as there are two Stones, or two Elixirs—one white and the other red—so there are two Arsenics which provide two souls, both the white and the red. Having these three materials, you must purify them perfectly, then conjoin them, and enclose them in the Vessel, and guide the work. The Sulphur of Antimony will fix the spirit of Vitriol, and the Arsenic will penetrate and hold them. I take Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the origins of alchemy and "hermetic" philosophy are attributed.</note> as my witness if ever anyone has spoken so clearly and truly, and those who will not believe me shall be fools.

...born from my thought. I see well that you desire to know the circumstances; my frankness, which made me begin to speak, commands me to tell you the rest.

One of these past nights, I was sleeping quite soundly; while sleeping, I was thinking of the secret. Good spirits are never idle, and often they do better while sleeping than while waking. I am made this way; I said to myself, "Is there no way to obtain this grace? God of Heaven, send me someone who favors my design; I feel a bit weak to arrive there by my readings and labors. I have already performed a thousand fooleries <note>original: "lanterneries". A term for trifling or nonsensical actions, often used to mock the failed experiments of amateur alchemists or "puffers."</note>; I have partitioned Gold, I have exposed it to the dew <note>Dew was highly valued in alchemy, believed to be a carrier of the "Universal Spirit" or celestial influence from the stars, often collected on sheets at dawn.</note> with Mercury, I have mixed it with my urines <note>Early experimenters frequently used urine to seek out salts and "animal spirits"; it was a common, if unpleasant, reagent in the quest for the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. <summary>The speaker describes his transition from a frustrated "puffer" performing failed experiments with dew and urine to seeking divine revelation through dreams. ### The Adventures of [the Unknown Philosopher] 102

...little I have put into practice, without ever seeing any sign of succeeding; I should no longer think of it, since it has made me forget God, but it is no longer in my power. This design has taken such a deep root in my soul that it would take a God to make me lose it; therefore, Lord of Heaven, take it from me, or give me the knowledge of it.

I had no sooner finished this discourse than I perceived the great God of the sea <note>The Roman god Neptune, who in alchemical allegory often represents the "Philosophical Sea" or the universal solvent required to dissolve metals.</note> coming, holding in his hand a trident, with which he struck roughly upon three distinct things that he had at his feet. Then he said to me, "Look at these three things; they are the materials of the secret you seek. See how my Trident has made them work; that shows you clearly enough what you must do."

These three things, Gentlemen, were Antimony <note>A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid. In alchemy, it was often called the "Grey Wolf" and was highly valued for its ability to purify gold.</note>, the sublimate <note>Usually referring to "corrosive sublimate" (mercuric chloride), a toxic compound produced through sublimation, used as a powerful reagent.</note>, and the Tartar <note>A salt (potassium bitartrate) found in the sediment of wine casks, used by alchemists as a source of "fixed" alkali or as a purifying agent.</note>. After he had struck, the Antimony became an impalpable powder, the sublimate a very subtle spi- <note>The text likely continues as "spirit" (esprit), referring to the volatile vapor or essence of the substance.</note>

-># Unknown Philosopher.<-

Gentlemen, here is one of the greatest secrets that a living man has ever uttered, <note>original: *Arrigite aures*</note> **prick up your ears**. You must expose our **Mercury** during the day to the rays of the **Sun**, and at night to the **Moon**; they are the two baths of nature, one dry and the other moist. It must remain there for three whole weeks; it is no great task, but who would have thought of it if I had not said it? Now, here is the reason. Mercury has two stains from which it must be washed: one watery, the other earthy. The watery one evaporates through the rays and heat of the Sun; the earthy one through the moist bath of the Moon. ### *Alchemist.*

Gentlemen,
Truth compels me to assert that in our <term>Mastery</term> <note>French: *Magistere*; the completion of the "Great Work" or the Philosopher's Stone</note> one must imitate nature, for the principles of nature are the principles of our <term>Art</term> <note>The "Art" is a common historical term for the practice of alchemy</note>; now, it is the case that common water and earth are the principles of nature; everything that exists owes the principle of its production to them. God made a body with mud, which was earth and water, then he inspired life <note>or "breathed in life"</note>; let us therefore take

       I have read the best Philosophers, and I hold the good **Morienus** <note>Morienus Romanus was a semi-legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist. <meta>This page continues the speech of the Tenth Alchemist, who began on the previous page. He is challenging the theories of his predecessors (who suggested gold, silver, minerals, or eggs) by arguing that the "First Matter" must be something produced by the human body and cast away.</meta>

<header>The Unknown Philosopher.</header>

Regarding **Gold**, not everyone carries it; for myself, it is enough to have a little **silver**. As for the other materials you have mentioned, we do not cast them out from ourselves, nor do we tread them underfoot. Has anyone ever seen a man produce **antimony** <note>A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid. In alchemy, it was often called the "Grey Wolf" because it was used to purify gold by "devouring" other metals.</note> or any other metal or mineral? As for the **Cock’s Egg** <note>A legendary object in alchemy, sometimes associated with the basilisk, but here used to mock the previous speaker's theory about literal eggs.</note>, has anyone ever laid one?

It is, therefore, something else that you do not know. I shall tell you; it is enough that I know it, since among us all things are held in common. To prove it to you, learn today that the **matter** is **spittle** <note>original: "crachat"; the Tenth Alchemist takes literally the philosophical claim that the "First Matter" is a vile thing found everywhere and cast out by the body.</note>. When we have spat, do we not walk upon it with our feet? Do we not cast it out through the mouth? This is exactly what **Morienus** says.

To show you that there is reason in this, I myself have **calcined** <note>To reduce a substance to a powder or "ash" by applying high heat, a fundamental step in the alchemical Great Work.</note> several metals using **spittle**. What greater proof could there be? Furthermore, everyone is well aware of the marvelous virtues possessed by spi—

# THE UNKNOWN PHILOSOPHER

# ADVICE OF THE ELEVENTH
## Alchemist.

### *Urine is the matter.*

Gentlemen,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is as well known as the Sun that the **matter** <note>The *Prima Materia* or "First Matter": the primordial, base substance that alchemists believed could be transformed into the Philosopher's Stone. It was famously described as being "found in the dung-heap" to emphasize its commonality.</note> is a vile thing; not only Morinus <note>Likely Jean-Baptiste Morin (1583–1656), a French mathematician, astrologer, and physician known for his works on natural philosophy.</note>, but all the Philosophers have written of it: it is found among dunghills, it is trodden underfoot, everyone casts it out from within themselves, and it is useful to everyone. I do not believe, however, that it is **spittle** <note>A direct rebuttal to the speaker on the previous page who proposed saliva as the secret ingredient.</note>, the use of which is neither beneficial nor common; if it produces any profitable effect at all, it is only for some small scratch that would have healed on its own with little effort. It is, much rather, **Urine** to which fits most properly everything that the Philosophers have described. Raymond Lully <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a celebrated Majorcan philosopher and mystic. Many influential alchemical texts were attributed to him, though most are now considered "Pseudo-Lullian" works written by followers.</note>, esteemed by us all, said it quite clearly: "urine of youth" <note>original: "vrina iuue-", likely a truncated form of *vrina iuvenum*. Medieval alchemical recipes frequently called for the urine of young boys, believed to be full of "vital spirits," as a source of ammonia or "mercury" for their experiments.</note>...

<summary>The Eleventh Alchemist argues that the secret "First Matter" of alchemy is not spittle, but human urine, citing its commonality and the authority of the philosopher Raymond Lully.</summary>
<keywords>First Matter, Prima Materia, Urine, Raymond Lully, Morinus, alchemical theory, youth's urine</keywords>

# THE ADVENTURES OF...

...of cholerics <note>original: "cholericorum." This completes the Latin phrase from the previous page, "vrina iuvenum cholericorum" (urine of young cholerics). In the theory of the Four Humors, a "choleric" person was thought to be governed by yellow bile, making them hot and dry—qualities alchemists associated with fire and gold.</note>, the urine of young cholerics. Even histories themselves report that formerly people have found flakes of fine gold in certain Urines: from this I draw the very necessary conclusion that in Urine resides the productive power of gold, seeing as one cannot give what one does not have within oneself.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Do we not also see that Urine engenders a stone <note>A reference to kidney stones or bladder stones (calculi). Alchemists often used biological "stones" as evidence that the body naturally performed a type of mineral transmutation.</note> within the kidneys? It can make one even richer being outside the kidneys, if it is guided by a wise hand and fermented **Philosophically** <note>In an alchemical context, "philosophically" means using the secret methods of the "true philosophers" (alchemists), such as controlled heating or specific distillation techniques.</note>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Do you wish then, Gentlemen, that we make the **Stone**? Let us take the Urine of young men of a choleric nature: I have a boy who is of this humor; I have made good arrangements to have the best wine in Europe. He will drink it entirely pure, as he is well accustomed to it; afterward we will have him urinate into the **Philosophical egg** <note>A term for an oval-shaped glass vessel or matrass, hermetically sealed, in which the "Philosophical Stone" was believed to hatch or mature during the long heating process.</note>, and we will have this **water of life** <note>original: "eau de vie." While this now refers to brandy, in alchemy, "aqua vitae" referred to a potent, rectified spirit or solvent believed to hold the essential spark of life.</note>,

Let us join these materials together, for what the **Philosophers** <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the Alchemists, often called "Philosophers of Fire."</note> have written could only apply to a combination of many, and I am certain that we shall succeed.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As he finished his discourse, everyone applauded him with great **praise**, and each individual offered to supply something. Hearing this, I would have willingly supplied the **urine**, for I was pissing my breeches with laughter, to such an extent that the old **President** of the whole assembly noticed quite well that I was laughing at them. But how could one help it? I believe that **Wisdom** herself, on such an occasion, would have laughed just like a madwoman. Nevertheless, they took it very poorly; they said among themselves: "Look at this stranger; we permitted him to be an auditor of our se-" <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing as "secrets" on the following page.</note>

As for the first point, to give yourself absolutely to God and to serve Him with a fervent heart, what could be more reasonable? Does not reason ordain that each thing be returned to the one to whom it belongs? Even the most barbarian nations observe this Law. Now, do you not know that you belong entirely to God? The creature <note>original: "la creature." Here meaning any created being, specifically a human being as a product of divine creation.</note> is entirely in the possession of the one who made it; thus, in serving God with all your heart, you are doing nothing other than following reason. What pain, then, can you imagine for yourself? Are you not a man? You are therefore reasonable <note>original: "raiſonnable." In the 17th century, this meant "endowed with the faculty of reason" or "rational."</note>, and your own nature is to act in conformity with reason. suffers pain while performing the actions that its nature requires? Far from it; the pleasure of every thing, as well as its perfection, lies in acting in such a manner. It is therefore not only easy, but agreeable, to give yourself to God, as it is an action in conformity with reason; consequently, it is natural to serve Him with heart and fervor.

As for the second point, which commands you to renounce vengeance, reserving it for God, it is also highly reasonable. A Parliament <note>original: "Parlement." In 17th-century France, a Parlement was a high court of justice rather than a legislative body.</note> does not pass judgment outside its jurisdiction <note>original: "ressort." A legal term referring to the specific territory or range of authority belonging to a court.</note>; reason and the Laws forbid it. All men fall under the jurisdiction of Heaven; it belongs to God alone to hear their cases. Do not be so bold as to encroach upon His rights.

If it were permitted for every individual to take revenge for the wrongs they have received, what purpose would Parliaments, Magistrates, and Judges serve in the world? Even if it were permitted, would you want to demean yourself by punishing him yourself—

# FIRST DISCOURSE
## OF PHILOSOPHY TO THE
### Unknown Philosopher, which declares the nature of the Stone, its effects, its excellences, along with its possibility and ease.

**M**y son,
Many seek the <term>Stone</term><note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical "Great Work," believed to be capable of perfecting both metals and the human soul.</note> and do not know what it is. The definitions that I know people give for it provide me with a subject for laughter and compassion. To explain the effects of a thing is not to define it; one must declare the nature that produces these effects, so as not to leave you in this er- <note>The word is cut off at the end of the page, likely intended to be "erreur" (error).</note>

# Unknown Philosopher.

world blames our secret, because it is entirely full of fools; it considers it impossible, because it loves ignorance.

I say even more: it is extremely easy; at least, it is not so difficult to find and to make as several arts that the common people practice. If men did not have the use of your glasses <note>Likely referring to lenses or glassware, which were sophisticated technologies at the time.</note>, who would believe them possible? If the way of making paper were not yet known, would men not judge it doubtful? It is the same with our **Stone**; it is not common, and therefore it is judged impossible or at least difficult; and yet it is easier to find and to make than paper, gunpowder, glass, and other things that common folk make as if in play. And certainly, it is not without reason that my children <note>In alchemical literature, "children" or "sons of art" refers to initiated students or fellow practitioners of the hermetic tradition.</note> call it the "play of women" <note>original: "ieu des femmes." Often known by the Latin term *ludus puerorum*, this phrase signifies that the final stages of the Great Work require little effort beyond maintaining a steady heat.</note>, seeing that a child instructed in the method can do it while laughing.

But where does it come from then, you will tell me, that of more than a hundred thousand who work at it exactly with great difficulty, only one will succeed? If it is so easy, why is it that for so many years that it has been sought with passion, only two or three have been fortunate in its possession? Why is it that so many good minds who astonished men with their subtlety <note>original: "subtilité." This refers to intellectual sharpness or the ability to grasp complex, hidden concepts.</note>, and by the invention of their rare wonders, have been stopped at a point that you judge easy? If they have pierced the Heavens <note>A reference to astronomers and mathematicians who, despite mapping the stars, remained "blind" to the fundamental secrets of nature on Earth.</note> with the point of their understanding, how have they been blind on the earth?

   At first glance this is astonishing; nonetheless, once two or three points are considered, you have no more reason to wonder. The Unknown Philosopher <note>original: "Philosophe Inconnu"</note>

<warning>Page has significant water staining/foxing in the center-right area, though the text remains legible.</warning>

For the reasons cited above, one requires the help of the Most High in this practice. The fact is that Heaven does not grant its aid to a man who is its enemy; one must possess a heart that is <term>purified: Meaning a heart cleansed of selfish motives or "stains" of sin through spiritual discipline.</term> and whole, stripped of worldly desires, and entirely devoted to God.

Ultimately, if most men possessed certain remedies to exempt or cure themselves of diseases, they would abandon themselves to vice; the fear of contracting some illness or of dying soon restrains them far more than the fear of God. But by possessing our <term>Stone: The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary alchemical substance capable of perfecting the human body and transmuting base metals into gold.</term>, one could preserve oneself and be healed from every <term>infirmity: A general term used in the 17th century for any physical weakness, disease, or chronic ailment.</term>—even those that our physicians label incurable.

This is why, my son, even though the Stone is possible and easy to attain, so few have the blessing of creating it.

I could give you yet more...

## SECOND DISCOURSE
### On the matter of the Stone.

It is at this point that everyone stumbles <note>original: "choppe," meaning to trip or fail.</note>; do you not know that all those who labor <note>Specifically referring to the "laborants" or practitioners working at the alchemical furnace.</note> all fixate upon different matters? It is, however, one of my <term>axioms: A fundamental principle or established rule that is accepted as true.</term> that the matter is one.

God willed to imprint His image within nature; just as He is one, and from this unity derive three persons <note>A reference to the Holy Trinity, suggesting that nature reflects the structure of its Creator.</note>, so too did He will that the matter be only one, and that from this unity should emerge the three kingdoms: the mineral, the vegetable, and the animal; the vegetable and the ani-

### The Unknown Philosopher

> “Being a <term>Hermaphrodite<note>In alchemical philosophy, the 'Hermaphrodite' or 'Rebis' represents a single substance that contains both the active (male) and passive (female) principles necessary for the Great Work.</note></term>, it is therefore male as well as female. This water is truly a <term>Mercury<note>Philosophical Mercury represents the principles of fluidity, volatility, and the "soul" of the matter; it is traditionally characterized as cold and moist.</note></term>; it is cold and moist, but it is also <term>Sulphur<note>Philosophical Sulphur represents the principles of heat, stability, and the "spirit"; it is characterized as hot and dry.</note></term>; it is dry and hot all at once. As Sulphur it is male; as Mercury it is female. As Sulphur, it heats and dries itself; as Mercury, it moistens and cools itself; and it acts as Sulphur so that the elements, being thus duly altered and mixed, reach the circle of the <term>Moon<note>The 'Moon' refers to the Albedo or 'White Work,' the stage where the matter reaches a state of crystalline purity.</note></term> if the Sulphur is white, or the Heaven of the <term>Sun<note>The 'Sun' refers to the Rubedo or 'Red Work,' the final stage of the process that produces the transformative Stone.</note></term> if it is red.

This being so, you possess three beautiful truths: the first, that the <term>Stone<note>The Philosopher’s Stone, the goal of alchemy, believed to be capable of transmuting base metals into gold and providing an elixir of life.</note></term> is possible; the second, that its matter is a single thing; and the third, that it is water, and <insert>water of our mineral,</insert> it remains for me to declare to you how it is made.

<summary>This page explains the dual nature of the alchemical prime matter, describing it as a "Hermaphrodite" that combines the opposing qualities of Mercury and Sulphur to reach the White or Red stages of the Great Work. It concludes by asserting the possibility of the Philosopher's Stone and confirming that its base is a single, mineral-derived water.</summary>

## THIRD DISCOURSE
### Concerning the Method of making the Philosopher's Stone.

My son, it is a great thing to know the matter upon which one wishes to perform a work, but it is not enough if one is ignorant of the method. You know well that a piece of wood is the matter for making a figure, yet you would not know how to craft it [without the skill]; likewise, knowing the matter of which our Stone is composed is a great advantage, but it is useless if one does not know how it must be guided. I have taught you the one; I wish to teach you the other.

You must first know that our water or matter has body, soul, and spirit, insofar as our <term>Stone<note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary catalyst of alchemy.</note></term> must possess <term>fixation<note>The quality of a substance that allows it to remain stable and non-volatile when exposed to high heat.</note></term> and <term>tincture<note>The transformative power or "dye" of the Stone that allows it to project its properties onto base metals.</note></term>, abundant in either the <term>white<note>The 'Albedo' or white stage, used to transmute metals into silver.</note></term> or the <term>red<note>The 'Rubedo' or red stage, used to transmute metals into gold.</note></term>, and furthermore <term>subtlety<note>The refined, penetrating state of matter that has been purified of its coarse elements.</note></term> and <term>fusion<note>The ability of the Stone to melt and flow like wax, allowing it to join with and transform other metals.</note></term> in order to fix and to dye, and

<note>original vocabulary: Philosopher's Stone, fixation, tincture, white, red, subtlety, fusion</note>

<summary>In this discourse, the teacher explains that identifying the alchemical matter is insufficient without understanding the process of its "guidance," introducing the essential triad of body, soul, and spirit.</summary>

Treatise of the Unknown Philosopher <note>original: "Philosophe Inconnu." This was the pseudonym of Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin, though the text here reflects earlier alchemical traditions he was associated with.</note> contained in this Book
</margin>

the Raven, which is the mark of a perfect <term>corruption<note>original: "corruption." In alchemy, this is the stage of putrefaction or "nigredo," where the matter must "die" and turn black before it can be reborn in a purified state.</note></term>; for art desires to kindle a true <term>tincture<note>original: "teintures." A tincture is a concentrated essence or spiritual force capable of "dyeing" or transforming the inner nature and color of a substance.</note></term> of either white or red, by means of the <term>soul<note>original: "l'ame." Alchemists often viewed the "soul" of the matter as its most subtle, volatile principle—its internal fire or sulfur.</note></term> which, being air and fire, tints in white and in red, having the whiteness of the air and the redness of fire. Now, art cannot communicate these two tinctures except after having exterminated the others, and arrived at the <term>blackest black<note>original: "noir tres noir." The peak of the nigredo stage, often symbolized by the head of a crow.</note></term>, under which is the white, and under the white the red; for you cannot pass to an extreme without passing through a middle, nor rise to a very high degree without passing through the lowest. Consider then in what corruption consists: extinguish the foreign colors by the diverse alteration and mixture of the elements; make them wear **mourning** as a mark of death, which will be for you a sure indicator of an upcoming life. Corrupt boldly so that you may engender; this operation is not too dangerous, take care only not to hur— <note>original: "pres-." Likely the beginning of "presser" (to hurry/press), suggesting the artist must not rush the slow process of putrefaction.</note>

### GENERATION.

<margin>fire</margin>
When you see the **Raven’s head** <note>original: "la teste du Corbeau." This marks the absolute end of the *nigredo* (black stage). In alchemical symbolism, "decapitating" the raven means moving past the state of decay.</note>, take our **cutlass**, for it must be cut off, and the **Dove** <note>original: "la Colombe." The symbol for the *albedo* (white stage), representing purification and the "whitening" of the matter.</note> put in its place. To do this, you need only to **circulate the elements** <term>circulate the elements<note>original: "circuler les elements." A central alchemical technique of repeated evaporation and condensation (distillation) intended to refine the substance by moving it through different physical states.</note></term>, so that the earth is made air by means of the water, and having returned to earth through a wise regime, the body becomes subtle. This occurs in the spirit, through which and in which the earth is refined and passed through its circulation; by this means, the most subtle water is converted into earth, and foreign colors are extinguished, so that there appears in the dried earth either the **white** or the **red**, by means of aerial or fiery heat. It is for this reason that I call this part **generation**, since the <term>mineral virtue<note>original: "vertu minerale." The internal essence or "seed" of the mineral that possesses the power to grow and transform into gold or the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term> is made strong and powerful by

<summary>This page describes the transition from the black stage of 'Corruption' to the stage of 'Generation,' where the 'Raven's head' is replaced by the 'Dove' through the circulation of elements. This process purifies the matter, leading it toward the final white or red states.</summary>
<keywords>generation, Raven's head, Dove, circulation of elements, mineral virtue, albedo, transmutation</keywords>

<margin><unclear>as</unclear> <note>original: "vt." Latin for "as" or "so that."</note></margin>
Take then, my son, the **Raven’s head** <note>original: "la teste du Corbeau." A traditional alchemical symbol for the black stage (*nigredo*), representing the stage of rot or "death" from which new life grows.</note>, and by decoction <term>decoction<note>The process of boiling a substance in a liquid to extract its essence.</note></term> increasing the fire by some degree, strip away its blackness; when you see that the blackness begins to leave, cause the earth, by means of water, to be converted into air, and then let it return to earth. Having done this several times, let the air and the fire (the variegated and foreign colors having been extinguished) be ignited in this dry earth, like an impalpable powder, either white or red; for the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> that you must compose with this powder could not penetrate bodies if it did not possess the tincture and the subtlety.

## MULTIPLICATION.

When you have your tinted powder, either in white or in red,

<margin>Note Well</margin>
according to the medicine <note>In alchemy, the "medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the elixir in its potent state, capable of "healing" base metals into gold.</note> & the ferment, take <insert>a portion of it as you wish</insert> & cause the air, by means of <insert>the water</insert>, to be congealed in the earth, with the same water, and this several times, which I call <insert>binding & unbinding</insert> <note>original: "lier & deslier." This is the French equivalent of the Latin *solve et coagula*, the fundamental alchemical process of dissolving a solid into a liquid and then re-solidifying it to purify it.</note>, by dissolving and digesting often, so that the spirit is fixed within the body, and by making it more penetrating and more subtle, it gives it <insert>fusibility or inceration</insert> <term>inceration<note>original: "incréation" (likely a variant or misspelling of *incération*). This is the process of adding liquid to a dry substance until it reaches a soft, wax-like consistency, allowing it to melt and penetrate metals.</note></term>. Now, by this frequent digestion, the <insert>mineral virtue</insert> <term>mineral virtue<note>The inherent formative power or "seed" within the mineral kingdom that allows for the growth and transmutation of metals.</note></term> acquires a very great perfection, and the air or rather the fire acting through it in the dried earth increases <insert>the tincture & the fixation</insert>; so that our Stone might communicate both very abundantly to the imperfect metals; in a word, it is enough to know that the multiplication is performed by the very same means as the composition was made, and you must always multiply until you see your medicine flow

<summary>This page describes the refinement of the Philosopher's Stone through repeated cycles of "binding and unbinding," which grants the substance its power to penetrate and transform base metals. It explains that the process of increasing the Stone's power (Multiplication) follows the same logic as its initial creation.</summary>

<keywords>medicine, ferment, binding and unbinding, solve et coagula, inceration, mineral virtue, tincture, fixation, Philosopher's Stone, multiplication</keywords>

onto the red-hot iron without smoke. <note>This refers to the "test of the plate," where a small amount of the Philosopher's Stone is placed on heated metal; if it melts like wax without producing smoke, the "tincture" is considered fixed and perfect.</note>
   Now then, my son, are you satisfied with me? Do you desire more?
   Truly, Madam, if I were not satisfied by such a beautiful, clear, and ample discourse, I would surely be insatiable. It compels me to confess that I have nothing more to wish for in this life but a holy retreat, where I might thank and praise at leisure Him who has deigned to favor me with so many graces.
   Your intention is laudable; strive to accomplish it as soon as it will be possible for you. I would never have revealed my secret to you except to make you wiser and to bind you entirely to Heaven, leaving you nothing more to wish for on Earth. Since solitude is a most fitting means to this end, the choice you have made pleases me and silences my tongue from saying anything further. Farewell, my dear child; I pray to Heaven

# The Adventures of the...
<note>The full title is "The Adventures of the Unknown Philosopher."</note>

...that He bless you.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Once Wisdom had withdrawn, I returned to my lodgings quite content, pondering the means to spend the remainder of my days within some Hermitage, with the intention of sharing my happiness with a companion of fortune of mine, whom I could not help but love despite the various grievances he had caused me. However, I was prevented from this by a most extraordinary path, for reasons that regard for him compels me to keep silent; since then, I have taken a firm resolution never to speak to anyone in the world unless God inspires me to do so.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There, Gentlemen, are all my adventures in the search for the <term>Great Work</term> <note>original: "grand œuvre"; in alchemy, this refers to the "Magnum Opus," the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone which grants both material wealth and spiritual enlightenment.</note>. You may, if you wish, profit from them, without any need for me to write to you further. Farewell then, and

<summary>The narrator concludes his memoir by describing his desire for a secluded life of prayer and his decision to stop sharing his alchemical secrets with others, unless divinely inspired.</summary>
<keywords>Wisdom, Hermitage, Great Work, spiritual retreat, alchemy, silence</keywords>

The Unknown Philosopher. 255

...let me go into my solitude
to think of nothing more but dying,
to live in the eternal day,
and there find another Stone
infinitely richer and more joyful:
*For the Rock was Christ.* <note>original: "Petra autem erat Christus"; a scriptural reference to 1 Corinthians 10:4. The author concludes by shifting the meaning of the "Stone" from an alchemical substance (the Philosopher's Stone) to a spiritual foundation in Jesus Christ.</note>
